Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah, nice to meet you too. Well, honestly, I'm here because... well, my head just feels incredibly foggy lately. Like, really thick and foggy. I'm a personal care aide, you know, helping folks with their daily routines, making sure they're safe and comfortable, and it's a job where you really have to be on the ball. You can't just space out when people are relying on you. 

But lately, I've been sitting down to write up my client notes at the end of a shift—which I’ve done a thousand times, I mean, I went to college for a bit, I know how to write a basic daily report—and I'm just making these little, stupid mistakes. Just silly errors that I shouldn't be making. And it’s not just the notes, it’s kind of happening all day long. I’ll get up to grab a towel or a glass of water for one of my guys, and halfway down the hall, I just completely forget what I was about to do. I just stand there in the hallway, totally blank, trying to retrace my steps in my head. 

It’s just... it really isn't like me at all. I've always prided myself on being sharp and dependable for my clients, so this whole brain fog thing is really throwing me for a loop. I just figured I needed to come in and talk to somebody about it because I can't afford to be slipping up at work, you know?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, honestly, I have. It's... it's kind of hard to explain, but it's like this weight. Lately, I wake up in the morning, and before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, I just have this heavy, empty feeling sitting right here in my chest. It's like a physical heaviness, you know? And it just stays there. I mean, I still get up, I still go to work, I can get through my shift and smile at my clients and talk to people, make sure they're fed and bathed and all that. I can go through the motions perfectly fine on the outside. But inside? Inside, I just feel... sad. Just really sad and flat. Like all the color has just been washed out of everything. 

It’s strange because I’m usually a pretty upbeat guy, you know? I like to joke around with the folks I care for, try to brighten their day a bit, because a lot of them are pretty isolated. But lately, I’m just faking it. I’m just putting on this mask, and underneath it, there's just this constant, dull ache of sadness that I can't seem to shake. It’s exhausting, really, pretending to be okay when you just feel so hollow inside all day long.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, I was just thinking about this the other day. I used to really love working in my garden. I have this little patch out back, nothing huge, just some tomatoes, a few peppers, some marigolds to keep the bugs away. And usually, come spring, I'm out there every weekend, getting my hands dirty, pulling weeds. It used to be this really peaceful thing for me, you know? Just me and the dirt and the quiet. It was a great way to decompress after a long week of lifting folks and dealing with all the noise at work. 

But this year... I don't know. I look out the window at it, and it just looks like work. It looks like a chore. The weeds are getting high, and the neighbor even mentioned it the other day—just casually, you know, "Hey, garden's looking a little wild!"—and I just couldn't bring myself to care. I went out there with my trowel last Saturday, stood there for maybe five minutes, and just turned around and went back inside and sat on the couch. 

And it's not just the garden, either. I used to bowl in this Thursday night league with a couple of buddies from high school. We aren't very good, but it was an excuse to get out of the house, have a beer, shoot the breeze. The last three weeks, I've just texted them and said I was too tired or had a headache. And I didn't even have a headache. I just... the thought of putting on my shoes, driving down to the alley, making small talk... it just feels like climbing a mountain. I just don't have the energy for it, and even when I think about doing it, it just doesn't sound fun anymore. It just sounds exhausting. So yeah, I guess I have lost interest. It's like the volume got turned way down on all the things I used to actually look forward to.

It’s exhausting, really, pretending to be okay when you just feel so hollow inside all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or enjoyment in things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, I was just thinking about this the other day. I used to really love working in my garden. I have this little patch out back, nothing huge, just some tomatoes, a few peppers, some marigolds to keep the bugs away. And usually, come spring, I'm out there every weekend, getting my hands dirty, pulling weeds. It used to be this really peaceful thing for me, you know? Just me and the dirt and the quiet. It was a great way to decompress after a long week of lifting folks and dealing with all the noise at work. \n\nBut this year... I don't know. I look out the window at it, and it just looks like work. It looks like a chore. The weeds are getting high, and the neighbor even mentioned it the other day—just casually, you know, \"Hey, garden's looking a little wild!\"—and I just couldn't bring myself to care. I went out there with my trowel last Saturday, stood there for maybe five minutes, and just turned around and went back inside and sat on the couch. \n\nAnd it's not just the garden, either. I used to bowl in this Thursday night league with a couple of buddies from high school. We aren't very good, but it was an excuse to get out of the house, have a beer, shoot the breeze. The last three weeks, I've just texted them and said I was too tired or had a headache. And I didn't even have a headache. I just... the thought of putting on my shoes, driving down to the alley, making small talk... it just feels like climbing a mountain. I just don't have the energy for it, and even when I think about doing it, it just doesn't sound fun anymore. It just sounds exhausting. So yeah, I guess I have lost interest. It's like the volume got turned way down on all the things I used to actually look forward to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, honestly, my sleep has just been all over the place lately. It's really frustrating, to be honest with you. Like, I'll get into bed at my normal time, usually around 10:30 or 11:00, because I have to be up early for my morning clients, right? And I'll be so physically exhausted from being on my feet all day, lifting people, running around... but the second my head hits the pillow, it's like my brain just turns on. 

I just lie there, staring at the ceiling, and my mind just starts racing. And it's not even about anything important half the time. Mostly, it's just family stuff, you know? Like, I'll start worrying about my sister in Ohio, wondering if her kids are doing okay in school, or I'll think about my dad and whether I should call him more often, or I'll just start replaying some stupid argument I had with my brother five years ago. Just this endless loop of thoughts that I can't seem to shut off, no matter how hard I try to just focus on my breathing or whatever. 

And then, if I do finally manage to drift off—usually after tossing and turning for a couple of hours—I don't stay asleep. I've been waking up way before my alarm, like at 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And once my eyes open, that's it. I'm awake. I try to settle back down, I flip the pillow over to the cool side, I try to get comfortable, but I just can't get back to sleep. I just lie there in the dark, watching the clock tick closer and closer to when I actually have to get up, and I just feel so restless and wide awake, but also totally drained at the same time. It's just a really terrible cycle, and it's making everything else during the day feel ten times harder.

I just stand there in the hallway, totally blank, trying to retrace my steps in my head. \n\nIt’s just... it really isn't like me at all. I've always prided myself on being sharp and dependable for my clients, so this whole brain fog thing is really throwing me for a loop. I just figured I needed to come in and talk to somebody about it because I can't afford to be slipping up at work, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, honestly, I have. It's... it's kind of hard to explain, but it's like this weight. Lately, I wake up in the morning, and before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, I just have this heavy, empty feeling sitting right here in my chest. It's like a physical heaviness, you know? And it just stays there. I mean, I still get up, I still go to work, I can get through my shift and smile at my clients and talk to people, make sure they're fed and bathed and all that. I can go through the motions perfectly fine on the outside. But inside? Inside, I just feel... sad. Just really sad and flat. Like all the color has just been washed out of everything. \n\nIt’s strange because I’m usually a pretty upbeat guy, you know? I like to joke around with the folks I care for, try to brighten their day a bit, because a lot of them are pretty isolated. But lately, I’m just faking it. I’m just putting on this mask, and underneath it, there's just this constant, dull ache of sadness that I can't seem to shake. It’s exhausting, really, pretending to be okay when you just feel so hollow inside all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or enjoyment in things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, I was just thinking about this the other day. I used to really love working in my garden. I have this little patch out back, nothing huge, just some tomatoes, a few peppers, some marigolds to keep the bugs away. And usually, come spring, I'm out there every weekend, getting my hands dirty, pulling weeds. It used to be this really peaceful thing for me, you know? Just me and the dirt and the quiet. It was a great way to decompress after a long week of lifting folks and dealing with all the noise at work. \n\nBut this year... I don't know. I look out the window at it, and it just looks like work. It looks like a chore. The weeds are getting high, and the neighbor even mentioned it the other day—just casually, you know, \"Hey, garden's looking a little wild!\"—and I just couldn't bring myself to care. I went out there with my trowel last Saturday, stood there for maybe five minutes, and just turned around and went back inside and sat on the couch. \n\nAnd it's not just the garden, either. I used to bowl in this Thursday night league with a couple of buddies from high school. We aren't very good, but it was an excuse to get out of the house, have a beer, shoot the breeze. The last three weeks, I've just texted them and said I was too tired or had a headache. And I didn't even have a headache. I just... the thought of putting on my shoes, driving down to the alley, making small talk... it just feels like climbing a mountain. I just don't have the energy for it, and even when I think about doing it, it just doesn't sound fun anymore. It just sounds exhausting. So yeah, I guess I have lost interest. It's like the volume got turned way down on all the things I used to actually look forward to.\n\nDoctor: How have you been sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, my sleep has just been all over the place lately. It's really frustrating, to be honest with you. Like, I'll get into bed at my normal time, usually around 10:30 or 11:00, because I have to be up early for my morning clients, right? And I'll be so physically exhausted from being on my feet all day, lifting people, running around... but the second my head hits the pillow, it's like my brain just turns on. \n\nI just lie there, staring at the ceiling, and my mind just starts racing. And it's not even about anything important half the time. Mostly, it's just family stuff, you know? Like, I'll start worrying about my sister in Ohio, wondering if her kids are doing okay in school, or I'll think about my dad and whether I should call him more often, or I'll just start replaying some stupid argument I had with my brother five years ago. Just this endless loop of thoughts that I can't seem to shut off, no matter how hard I try to just focus on my breathing or whatever. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally manage to drift off—usually after tossing and turning for a couple of hours—I don't stay asleep. I've been waking up way before my alarm, like at 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And once my eyes open, that's it. I'm awake. I try to settle back down, I flip the pillow over to the cool side, I try to get comfortable, but I just can't get back to sleep. I just lie there in the dark, watching the clock tick closer and closer to when I actually have to get up, and I just feel so restless and wide awake, but also totally drained at the same time. It's just a really terrible cycle, and it's making everything else during the day feel ten times harder.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It's just a really terrible cycle, and it's making everything else during the day feel ten times harder.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been feeling this way?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / personal_care_aide / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressive Episode Duration\nmanifestation: \"The low, guilty, foggy feeling has been hanging over me for about a month now.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... let me think. It's been a little while now. I'd say it really started settling in about a month ago. Yeah, definitely around a month. I remember because it was right around the time I was supposed to go to that family barbecue my cousin hosts every year. Usually, I'm the one bringing the potato salad, making sure everyone's got a drink, you know, just being in the middle of it all. But this year, I just... I woke up that morning, and that heavy, foggy feeling was just there, weighing me down. I ended up calling and making some excuse about a stomach bug because I just couldn't face everyone. 

And it's just been hanging over me ever since. Every single day for the last four weeks or so. It's not like it comes and goes, either; it's just this constant, low-level hum of feeling guilty and foggy and just... down. I keep thinking, "Okay, tomorrow I'll wake up and feel like myself again," but tomorrow comes, and it's the exact same thing. A whole month of just dragging myself through the days, feeling like I'm moving through molasses. It's really starting to wear me out, honestly, because a month feels like a really long time when you're just feeling this low every single day.

Just silly errors that I shouldn't be making. And it’s not just the notes, it’s kind of happening all day long. Lately, I wake up in the morning, and before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, I just have this heavy, empty feeling sitting right here in my chest. It's like a physical heaviness, you know? And it just stays there. I mean, I still get up, I still go to work, I can get through my shift and smile at my clients and talk to people, make sure they're fed and bathed and all that. I can go through the motions perfectly fine on the outside. But inside? Inside, I just feel... sad. Just really sad and flat. Like all the color has just been washed out of everything. \n\nIt’s strange because I’m usually a pretty upbeat guy, you know? I like to joke around with the folks I care for, try to brighten their day a bit, because a lot of them are pretty isolated. But lately, I’m just faking it. I’m just putting on this mask, and underneath it, there's just this constant, dull ache of sadness that I can't seem to shake. It’s exhausting, really, pretending to be okay when you just feel so hollow inside all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or enjoyment in things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, I was just thinking about this the other day. I used to really love working in my garden. I have this little patch out back, nothing huge, just some tomatoes, a few peppers, some marigolds to keep the bugs away. And usually, come spring, I'm out there every weekend, getting my hands dirty, pulling weeds. It used to be this really peaceful thing for me, you know? Just me and the dirt and the quiet. It was a great way to decompress after a long week of lifting folks and dealing with all the noise at work. \n\nBut this year... I don't know. I look out the window at it, and it just looks like work. It looks like a chore. I went out there with my trowel last Saturday, stood there for maybe five minutes, and just turned around and went back inside and sat on the couch. \n\nAnd it's not just the garden, either. I used to bowl in this Thursday night league with a couple of buddies from high school. We aren't very good, but it was an excuse to get out of the house, have a beer, shoot the breeze. The last three weeks, I've just texted them and said I was too tired or had a headache. And I didn't even have a headache. I just... the thought of putting on my shoes, driving down to the alley, making small talk... it just feels like climbing a mountain. I just don't have the energy for it, and even when I think about doing it, it just doesn't sound fun anymore. It just sounds exhausting. So yeah, I guess I have lost interest. Just this endless loop of thoughts that I can't seem to shut off, no matter how hard I try to just focus on my breathing or whatever. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally manage to drift off—usually after tossing and turning for a couple of hours—I don't stay asleep. I've been waking up way before my alarm, like at 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And once my eyes open, that's it. I'm awake. I try to settle back down, I flip the pillow over to the cool side, I try to get comfortable, but I just can't get back to sleep. I just lie there in the dark, watching the clock tick closer and closer to when I actually have to get up, and I just feel so restless and wide awake, but also totally drained at the same time. I ended up calling and making some excuse about a stomach bug because I just couldn't face everyone. \n\nAnd it's just been hanging over me ever since. Every single day for the last four weeks or so. I keep thinking, \"Okay, tomorrow I'll wake up and feel like myself again,\" but tomorrow comes, and it's the exact same thing. A whole month of just dragging myself through the days, feeling like I'm moving through molasses. It's really starting to wear me out, honestly, because a month feels like a really long time when you're just feeling this low every single day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that's... that's a heavy question, I guess. It's hard to even say it out loud, to be honest. Um... no, I haven't... I haven't had any actual plans to hurt myself or anything like that. I want to be really clear about that, because I know how that sounds, and I'm not... I'm not sitting around trying to figure out a way to end things. I've got my clients who rely on me, and my family, even if I haven't been as present with them lately. 

But... and this is the part that really bothers me, the part that actually scares me a little bit when I really stop and think about it... there have been mornings lately. Like, I told you about how I wake up at 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning and just lie there in the dark? Well, sometimes, when I'm just lying there, watching the ceiling, feeling so exhausted but unable to sleep, and knowing I have to get up and face another day feeling this heavy, foggy way... the thought just kind of creeps in. 

It's not a loud thought, just this quiet little whisper that says, you know, "It really wouldn't be the worst thing in the world if I just... didn't wake up." Like, if I could just go back to sleep and stay asleep, and not have to deal with the exhaustion and the guilt and the feeling like everything is just too much effort. It's just this overwhelming desire for it all to just stop. For the quiet to just stay quiet. 

And then I'll catch myself thinking it, and I'll feel this jolt of panic, like, "Whoa, where did that come from? That's not me." Because I've always been a pretty resilient guy, you know? I've always pushed through. But lately, those mornings... it's just this passive feeling of wanting to be done with it all. Not wanting to kill myself, just... not wanting to be awake anymore. And having those thoughts, even just fleetingly, it really rattles me. It makes me feel like I'm losing my grip on things a little bit, and that's a really terrifying feeling when you're used to being the one holding everything together for everyone else.

Well, honestly... I wouldn't say I'm worrying more than usual. I mean, I told you about how my mind races when I'm trying to sleep, right? I don't really have the mental bandwidth left over to just sit and worry about things. 

So, yeah, no, I wouldn't say there's any extra worrying going on. Just the usual family stuff that always crosses my mind, just... maybe it just feels louder at night because I can't sleep, you know? I woke up that morning, and that heavy, foggy feeling was just there, weighing me down. I ended up calling and making some excuse about a stomach bug because I just couldn't face everyone. \n\nAnd it's just been hanging over me ever since. Every single day for the last four weeks or so. It's not like it comes and goes, either; it's just this constant, low-level hum of feeling guilty and foggy and just... down. I keep thinking, \"Okay, tomorrow I'll wake up and feel like myself again,\" but tomorrow comes, and it's the exact same thing. A whole month of just dragging myself through the days, feeling like I'm moving through molasses. It's really starting to wear me out, honestly, because a month feels like a really long time when you're just feeling this low every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a heavy question, I guess. It's hard to even say it out loud, to be honest. Um... no, I haven't... I haven't had any actual plans to hurt myself or anything like that. I want to be really clear about that, because I know how that sounds, and I'm not... I'm not sitting around trying to figure out a way to end things. I've got my clients who rely on me, and my family, even if I haven't been as present with them lately. \n\nBut... and this is the part that really bothers me, the part that actually scares me a little bit when I really stop and think about it... there have been mornings lately. Like, I told you about how I wake up at 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning and just lie there in the dark? Well, sometimes, when I'm just lying there, watching the ceiling, feeling so exhausted but unable to sleep, and knowing I have to get up and face another day feeling this heavy, foggy way... the thought just kind of creeps in. \n\nIt's not a loud thought, just this quiet little whisper that says, you know, \"It really wouldn't be the worst thing in the world if I just... didn't wake up.\" Like, if I could just go back to sleep and stay asleep, and not have to deal with the exhaustion and the guilt and the feeling like everything is just too much effort. It's just this overwhelming desire for it all to just stop. For the quiet to just stay quiet. \n\nAnd then I'll catch myself thinking it, and I'll feel this jolt of panic, like, \"Whoa, where did that come from? That's not me.\" Because I've always been a pretty resilient guy, you know? I've always pushed through. But lately, those mornings... it's just this passive feeling of wanting to be done with it all. Not wanting to kill myself, just... not wanting to be awake anymore. And having those thoughts, even just fleetingly, it really rattles me. It makes me feel like I'm losing my grip on things a little bit, and that's a really terrifying feeling when you're used to being the one holding everything together for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been worrying more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly... I don't really have the mental bandwidth left over to just sit and worry about things. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I wouldn't say there's any extra worrying going on. Well, sometimes, when I'm just lying there, watching the ceiling, feeling so exhausted but unable to sleep, and knowing I have to get up and face another day feeling this heavy, foggy way... the thought just kind of creeps in. \n\nIt's not a loud thought, just this quiet little whisper that says, you know, \"It really wouldn't be the worst thing in the world if I just... didn't wake up.\" Like, if I could just go back to sleep and stay asleep, and not have to deal with the exhaustion and the guilt and the feeling like everything is just too much effort. It's just this overwhelming desire for it all to just stop. For the quiet to just stay quiet. \n\nAnd then I'll catch myself thinking it, and I'll feel this jolt of panic, like, \"Whoa, where did that come from? That's not me.\" Because I've always been a pretty resilient guy, you know? I've always pushed through. But lately, those mornings... it's just this passive feeling of wanting to be done with it all. Not wanting to kill myself, just... not wanting to be awake anymore. And having those thoughts, even just fleetingly, it really rattles me. It makes me feel like I'm losing my grip on things a little bit, and that's a really terrifying feeling when you're used to being the one holding everything together for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been worrying more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly... I wouldn't say I'm worrying more than usual. I mean, I told you about how my mind races when I'm trying to sleep, right? Thinking about my sister in Ohio and her kids, or my dad, or whatever. But that's just... that's just normal stuff. It's not like I'm sitting around all day chewing my fingernails off about things. \n\nIt's more just that when I'm lying there in the dark, and it's so quiet, those are the thoughts that just kind of pop up and loop around. During the day, I'm just so focused on getting through the fog and the exhaustion. I'm busy with my clients—helping Mr. Henderson with his physical therapy exercises, making sure Mrs. Gable takes her meds on time, getting them bathed and fed. That takes up all my energy. I don't really have the mental bandwidth left over to just sit and worry about things. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I wouldn't say there's any extra worrying going on. Just the usual family stuff that always crosses my mind, just... maybe it just feels louder at night because I can't sleep, you know? But during the day, no, it's just the heaviness and the being tired. That's what's really taking over everything right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow has your appetite been lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / personal_care_aide / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my appetite... you know, I haven't really noticed any big changes there. I mean, I'm still eating. I have to eat, right? Especially with the work I do. Being a personal care aide, you're on your feet, you're lifting people, you're moving around constantly. If I don't eat, I just wouldn't have the physical strength to help Mr. Henderson out of his chair, and he's not a small guy. 

So, I still make myself breakfast, usually just some toast and coffee, maybe a scrambled egg if I can muster the energy, which, like I said, has been really tough lately with how heavy everything feels. But I force it down. And for lunch, I usually pack a sandwich or heat up some soup while I'm at Mrs. Gable's house. It's nothing fancy, just fuel to keep me going. Dinner is pretty much the same deal. Sometimes I'll just grab something quick on the way home or throw a frozen dinner in the microwave because by the time I get back to my place, I'm just so drained from dragging myself through the day. 

But as far as being hungrier or losing my appetite completely? No, I wouldn't say that's happening. Food just kind of tastes the same as it always does, it's just... more of a chore to actually prepare it and eat it right now. But I'm definitely still eating my regular meals, just going through the motions with it, really. It's just one more thing on the list of things I have to force myself to do every day, you know? But the actual amount I'm eating, or how hungry I feel... that hasn't really changed at all.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, nice to meet you too. Well, honestly, I'm here because... well, my head just feels incredibly foggy lately. Like, really thick and foggy. I'm a personal care aide, you know, helping folks with their daily routines, making sure they're safe and comfortable, and it's a job where you really have to be on the ball. You can't just space out when people are relying on you. \n\nBut lately, I've been sitting down to write up my client notes at the end of a shift—which I’ve done a thousand times, I mean, I went to college for a bit, I know how to write a basic daily report—and I'm just making these little, stupid mistakes. Just silly errors that I shouldn't be making. And it’s not just the notes, it’s kind of happening all day long. I’ll get up to grab a towel or a glass of water for one of my guys, and halfway down the hall, I just completely forget what I was about to do. I just stand there in the hallway, totally blank, trying to retrace my steps in my head. \n\nIt’s just... it really isn't like me at all. I've always prided myself on being sharp and dependable for my clients, so this whole brain fog thing is really throwing me for a loop. I just figured I needed to come in and talk to somebody about it because I can't afford to be slipping up at work, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, honestly, I have. It's... it's kind of hard to explain, but it's like this weight. Lately, I wake up in the morning, and before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, I just have this heavy, empty feeling sitting right here in my chest. It's like a physical heaviness, you know? And it just stays there. I mean, I still get up, I still go to work, I can get through my shift and smile at my clients and talk to people, make sure they're fed and bathed and all that. I can go through the motions perfectly fine on the outside. But inside? Inside, I just feel... sad. Just really sad and flat. Like all the color has just been washed out of everything. \n\nIt’s strange because I’m usually a pretty upbeat guy, you know? I like to joke around with the folks I care for, try to brighten their day a bit, because a lot of them are pretty isolated. But lately, I’m just faking it. I’m just putting on this mask, and underneath it, there's just this constant, dull ache of sadness that I can't seem to shake. It’s exhausting, really, pretending to be okay when you just feel so hollow inside all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or enjoyment in things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, I was just thinking about this the other day. I used to really love working in my garden. I have this little patch out back, nothing huge, just some tomatoes, a few peppers, some marigolds to keep the bugs away. And usually, come spring, I'm out there every weekend, getting my hands dirty, pulling weeds. It used to be this really peaceful thing for me, you know? Just me and the dirt and the quiet. It was a great way to decompress after a long week of lifting folks and dealing with all the noise at work. \n\nBut this year... I don't know. I look out the window at it, and it just looks like work. It looks like a chore. The weeds are getting high, and the neighbor even mentioned it the other day—just casually, you know, \"Hey, garden's looking a little wild!\"—and I just couldn't bring myself to care. I went out there with my trowel last Saturday, stood there for maybe five minutes, and just turned around and went back inside and sat on the couch. \n\nAnd it's not just the garden, either. I used to bowl in this Thursday night league with a couple of buddies from high school. We aren't very good, but it was an excuse to get out of the house, have a beer, shoot the breeze. The last three weeks, I've just texted them and said I was too tired or had a headache. And I didn't even have a headache. I just... the thought of putting on my shoes, driving down to the alley, making small talk... it just feels like climbing a mountain. I just don't have the energy for it, and even when I think about doing it, it just doesn't sound fun anymore. It just sounds exhausting. So yeah, I guess I have lost interest. It's like the volume got turned way down on all the things I used to actually look forward to.\n\nDoctor: How have you been sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, my sleep has just been all over the place lately. It's really frustrating, to be honest with you. Like, I'll get into bed at my normal time, usually around 10:30 or 11:00, because I have to be up early for my morning clients, right? And I'll be so physically exhausted from being on my feet all day, lifting people, running around... but the second my head hits the pillow, it's like my brain just turns on. \n\nI just lie there, staring at the ceiling, and my mind just starts racing. And it's not even about anything important half the time. Mostly, it's just family stuff, you know? Like, I'll start worrying about my sister in Ohio, wondering if her kids are doing okay in school, or I'll think about my dad and whether I should call him more often, or I'll just start replaying some stupid argument I had with my brother five years ago. Just this endless loop of thoughts that I can't seem to shut off, no matter how hard I try to just focus on my breathing or whatever. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally manage to drift off—usually after tossing and turning for a couple of hours—I don't stay asleep. I've been waking up way before my alarm, like at 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And once my eyes open, that's it. I'm awake. I try to settle back down, I flip the pillow over to the cool side, I try to get comfortable, but I just can't get back to sleep. I just lie there in the dark, watching the clock tick closer and closer to when I actually have to get up, and I just feel so restless and wide awake, but also totally drained at the same time. It's just a really terrible cycle, and it's making everything else during the day feel ten times harder.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... let me think. It's been a little while now. I'd say it really started settling in about a month ago. Yeah, definitely around a month. I remember because it was right around the time I was supposed to go to that family barbecue my cousin hosts every year. Usually, I'm the one bringing the potato salad, making sure everyone's got a drink, you know, just being in the middle of it all. But this year, I just... I woke up that morning, and that heavy, foggy feeling was just there, weighing me down. I ended up calling and making some excuse about a stomach bug because I just couldn't face everyone. \n\nAnd it's just been hanging over me ever since. Every single day for the last four weeks or so. It's not like it comes and goes, either; it's just this constant, low-level hum of feeling guilty and foggy and just... down. I keep thinking, \"Okay, tomorrow I'll wake up and feel like myself again,\" but tomorrow comes, and it's the exact same thing. A whole month of just dragging myself through the days, feeling like I'm moving through molasses. It's really starting to wear me out, honestly, because a month feels like a really long time when you're just feeling this low every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a heavy question, I guess. I've got my clients who rely on me, and my family, even if I haven't been as present with them lately. \n\nBut... and this is the part that really bothers me, the part that actually scares me a little bit when I really stop and think about it... there have been mornings lately. For the quiet to just stay quiet. \n\nAnd then I'll catch myself thinking it, and I'll feel this jolt of panic, like, \"Whoa, where did that come from? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, actually, that's... that's a big part of why I decided I needed to come in and talk to somebody, to be honest. It's like I was saying earlier about that heavy, foggy feeling? It's not just physical exhaustion, it's... my actual head feels foggy. Like my brain is wrapped in thick cotton or something, and I just can't think straight. 

It's starting to show up at work, which really bothers me. As a personal care aide, I have to keep these daily logs, these client notes for the agency. It's really important stuff—what time they ate, exactly how many milligrams of their medication they took, their vitals, all those daily living activities. And I've always been incredibly meticulous about it. I have to be, you know? But lately... I'm making these stupid little mistakes in the logs. I'll write down the wrong time, or I'll catch myself writing Tuesday's date when it's Thursday, or I'll have to sit there staring at the paper for five whole minutes just trying to remember if I gave Mrs. Gable her water pill at 9:00 or 9:30. I mean, I always figure it out eventually, I double-check the pillbox, but it's like wading through mud just to pull up a simple memory that should just be right there.

And it's not just the paperwork, either. It's the moment-to-moment stuff. I'll be in the middle of doing something, like... I'll walk from Mr. Henderson's living room into his kitchen because I know I need to grab something, maybe a fresh dish towel or a glass of water for him, and I'll get into the kitchen and just stop dead. Just completely blank. I'll stand there looking at the fridge, looking at the counter, and I have absolutely no idea what I walked in there for. I literally have to walk all the way back into the living room and look at him to trigger the memory of what I was about to do. 

And that... that just isn't like me at all. I'm the guy who remembers everyone's schedule, everyone's appointments, who likes what on their sandwich. I usually have a mind like a steel trap because in my line of work, you really can't afford to be scatterbrained. People are relying on you. So having this constant fog, making these little errors, forgetting what I'm doing right in the middle of doing it... it's incredibly frustrating. It makes me feel like I can't trust my own brain right now.

You can't just space out when people are relying on you. \n\nBut lately, I've been sitting down to write up my client notes at the end of a shift—which I’ve done a thousand times, I mean, I went to college for a bit, I know how to write a basic daily report—and I'm just making these little, stupid mistakes. Just silly errors that I shouldn't be making. And it’s not just the notes, it’s kind of happening all day long. I’ll get up to grab a towel or a glass of water for one of my guys, and halfway down the hall, I just completely forget what I was about to do. I just stand there in the hallway, totally blank, trying to retrace my steps in my head. \n\nIt’s just... it really isn't like me at all. I've always prided myself on being sharp and dependable for my clients, so this whole brain fog thing is really throwing me for a loop. I just figured I needed to come in and talk to somebody about it because I can't afford to be slipping up at work, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, honestly, I have. It's... it's kind of hard to explain, but it's like this weight. Lately, I wake up in the morning, and before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, I just have this heavy, empty feeling sitting right here in my chest. It's like a physical heaviness, you know? And it just stays there. I mean, I still get up, I still go to work, I can get through my shift and smile at my clients and talk to people, make sure they're fed and bathed and all that. I can go through the motions perfectly fine on the outside. But inside? Inside, I just feel... sad. Just really sad and flat. Like all the color has just been washed out of everything. \n\nIt’s strange because I’m usually a pretty upbeat guy, you know? I like to joke around with the folks I care for, try to brighten their day a bit, because a lot of them are pretty isolated. But lately, I’m just faking it. I’m just putting on this mask, and underneath it, there's just this constant, dull ache of sadness that I can't seem to shake. It’s exhausting, really, pretending to be okay when you just feel so hollow inside all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or enjoyment in things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, I was just thinking about this the other day. I used to really love working in my garden. I have this little patch out back, nothing huge, just some tomatoes, a few peppers, some marigolds to keep the bugs away. And usually, come spring, I'm out there every weekend, getting my hands dirty, pulling weeds. It used to be this really peaceful thing for me, you know? Just me and the dirt and the quiet. It was a great way to decompress after a long week of lifting folks and dealing with all the noise at work. \n\nBut this year... I don't know. I look out the window at it, and it just looks like work. It looks like a chore. The weeds are getting high, and the neighbor even mentioned it the other day—just casually, you know, \"Hey, garden's looking a little wild!\"—and I just couldn't bring myself to care. I went out there with my trowel last Saturday, stood there for maybe five minutes, and just turned around and went back inside and sat on the couch. \n\nAnd it's not just the garden, either. I used to bowl in this Thursday night league with a couple of buddies from high school. We aren't very good, but it was an excuse to get out of the house, have a beer, shoot the breeze. The last three weeks, I've just texted them and said I was too tired or had a headache. And I didn't even have a headache. I just... the thought of putting on my shoes, driving down to the alley, making small talk... it just feels like climbing a mountain. I just don't have the energy for it, and even when I think about doing it, it just doesn't sound fun anymore. It just sounds exhausting. So yeah, I guess I have lost interest. It's like the volume got turned way down on all the things I used to actually look forward to.\n\nDoctor: How have you been sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, my sleep has just been all over the place lately. It's really frustrating, to be honest with you. Like, I'll get into bed at my normal time, usually around 10:30 or 11:00, because I have to be up early for my morning clients, right? And I'll be so physically exhausted from being on my feet all day, lifting people, running around... but the second my head hits the pillow, it's like my brain just turns on. \n\nI just lie there, staring at the ceiling, and my mind just starts racing. And it's not even about anything important half the time. Mostly, it's just family stuff, you know? Like, I'll start worrying about my sister in Ohio, wondering if her kids are doing okay in school, or I'll think about my dad and whether I should call him more often, or I'll just start replaying some stupid argument I had with my brother five years ago. Just this endless loop of thoughts that I can't seem to shut off, no matter how hard I try to just focus on my breathing or whatever. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally manage to drift off—usually after tossing and turning for a couple of hours—I don't stay asleep. I've been waking up way before my alarm, like at 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And once my eyes open, that's it. I'm awake. I try to settle back down, I flip the pillow over to the cool side, I try to get comfortable, but I just can't get back to sleep. I just lie there in the dark, watching the clock tick closer and closer to when I actually have to get up, and I just feel so restless and wide awake, but also totally drained at the same time. It's just a really terrible cycle, and it's making everything else during the day feel ten times harder.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... let me think. It's been a little while now. I'd say it really started settling in about a month ago. Yeah, definitely around a month. I remember because it was right around the time I was supposed to go to that family barbecue my cousin hosts every year. Usually, I'm the one bringing the potato salad, making sure everyone's got a drink, you know, just being in the middle of it all. But this year, I just... I woke up that morning, and that heavy, foggy feeling was just there, weighing me down. I ended up calling and making some excuse about a stomach bug because I just couldn't face everyone. \n\nAnd it's just been hanging over me ever since. Every single day for the last four weeks or so. It's not like it comes and goes, either; it's just this constant, low-level hum of feeling guilty and foggy and just... down. I keep thinking, \"Okay, tomorrow I'll wake up and feel like myself again,\" but tomorrow comes, and it's the exact same thing. A whole month of just dragging myself through the days, feeling like I'm moving through molasses. It's really starting to wear me out, honestly, because a month feels like a really long time when you're just feeling this low every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a heavy question, I guess. It's hard to even say it out loud, to be honest. Um... no, I haven't... I haven't had any actual plans to hurt myself or anything like that. I want to be really clear about that, because I know how that sounds, and I'm not... I'm not sitting around trying to figure out a way to end things. I've got my clients who rely on me, and my family, even if I haven't been as present with them lately. \n\nBut... and this is the part that really bothers me, the part that actually scares me a little bit when I really stop and think about it... there have been mornings lately. Like, I told you about how I wake up at 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning and just lie there in the dark? Well, sometimes, when I'm just lying there, watching the ceiling, feeling so exhausted but unable to sleep, and knowing I have to get up and face another day feeling this heavy, foggy way... the thought just kind of creeps in. \n\nIt's not a loud thought, just this quiet little whisper that says, you know, \"It really wouldn't be the worst thing in the world if I just... didn't wake up.\" Like, if I could just go back to sleep and stay asleep, and not have to deal with the exhaustion and the guilt and the feeling like everything is just too much effort. It's just this overwhelming desire for it all to just stop. For the quiet to just stay quiet. \n\nAnd then I'll catch myself thinking it, and I'll feel this jolt of panic, like, \"Whoa, where did that come from? That's not me.\" Because I've always been a pretty resilient guy, you know? I've always pushed through. But lately, those mornings... it's just this passive feeling of wanting to be done with it all. Not wanting to kill myself, just... not wanting to be awake anymore. And having those thoughts, even just fleetingly, it really rattles me. It makes me feel like I'm losing my grip on things a little bit, and that's a really terrifying feeling when you're used to being the one holding everything together for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been worrying more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly... I wouldn't say I'm worrying more than usual. I mean, I told you about how my mind races when I'm trying to sleep, right? Thinking about my sister in Ohio and her kids, or my dad, or whatever. But that's just... that's just normal stuff. It's not like I'm sitting around all day chewing my fingernails off about things. \n\nIt's more just that when I'm lying there in the dark, and it's so quiet, those are the thoughts that just kind of pop up and loop around. During the day, I'm just so focused on getting through the fog and the exhaustion. I'm busy with my clients—helping Mr. Henderson with his physical therapy exercises, making sure Mrs. Gable takes her meds on time, getting them bathed and fed. That takes up all my energy. I don't really have the mental bandwidth left over to just sit and worry about things. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I wouldn't say there's any extra worrying going on. Just the usual family stuff that always crosses my mind, just... maybe it just feels louder at night because I can't sleep, you know? But during the day, no, it's just the heaviness and the being tired. That's what's really taking over everything right now.\n\nDoctor: How has your appetite been lately?\n\nPatient: Well, my appetite... you know, I haven't really noticed any big changes there. I mean, I'm still eating. I have to eat, right? Especially with the work I do. Being a personal care aide, you're on your feet, you're lifting people, you're moving around constantly. If I don't eat, I just wouldn't have the physical strength to help Mr. Henderson out of his chair, and he's not a small guy. \n\nSo, I still make myself breakfast, usually just some toast and coffee, maybe a scrambled egg if I can muster the energy, which, like I said, has been really tough lately with how heavy everything feels. But I force it down. And for lunch, I usually pack a sandwich or heat up some soup while I'm at Mrs. Gable's house. It's nothing fancy, just fuel to keep me going. Dinner is pretty much the same deal. Sometimes I'll just grab something quick on the way home or throw a frozen dinner in the microwave because by the time I get back to my place, I'm just so drained from dragging myself through the day. \n\nBut as far as being hungrier or losing my appetite completely? But the actual amount I'm eating, or how hungry I feel... that hasn't really changed at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, that's... that's a big part of why I decided I needed to come in and talk to somebody, to be honest. Like my brain is wrapped in thick cotton or something, and I just can't think straight. \n\nIt's starting to show up at work, which really bothers me. As a personal care aide, I have to keep these daily logs, these client notes for the agency. And I've always been incredibly meticulous about it. I have to be, you know? But lately... I'm making these stupid little mistakes in the logs. I'll write down the wrong time, or I'll catch myself writing Tuesday's date when it's Thursday, or I'll have to sit there staring at the paper for five whole minutes just trying to remember if I gave Mrs. Gable her water pill at 9:00 or 9:30. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, honestly... I don't think I'm moving or talking slower. I mean, I know I've been talking a lot about how heavy everything feels, that physical exhaustion I mentioned, where it just takes so much effort to get out of bed or make breakfast. But once I'm actually up and moving, especially at work, I have to keep my normal pace. 

Like with Mr. Henderson, for example. When we do his physical therapy exercises, there's a specific rhythm to it. If I move too slow, he loses his balance, and if I talk too slow while counting out his reps, he gets confused and frustrated. So I'm still counting, "One, two, three, lift," at the exact same speed I always have. And with Mrs. Gable, she's a bit hard of hearing, so I have to speak up and speak clearly, but I'm not, you know, dragging my words out or sounding like a record played at the wrong speed. 

And as for moving around... I'm still doing the laundry, still getting the meals prepared, still helping them transfer from the bed to the wheelchair. I might feel completely drained on the inside, like I'm running on empty, but on the outside, my body is still doing the motions at the same speed. It's not like I'm walking through molasses or anything. It's just that the *effort* to move at that normal speed is taking a lot more out of me. But if you were just watching me work, or listening to me talk to my clients—or even just talking to you right now—I don't think you'd say, "Wow, he's really moving in slow motion." So, no, I wouldn't say there's any actual slowness in my movements or my speech, just the internal heaviness.

But once I'm actually up and moving, especially at work, I have to keep my normal pace. \n\nLike with Mr. Henderson, for example. When we do his physical therapy exercises, there's a specific rhythm to it. If I move too slow, he loses his balance, and if I talk too slow while counting out his reps, he gets confused and frustrated. So I'm still counting, \"One, two, three, lift,\" at the exact same speed I always have. And with Mrs. Gable, she's a bit hard of hearing, so I have to speak up and speak clearly, but I'm not, you know, dragging my words out or sounding like a record played at the wrong speed. \n\nAnd as for moving around... I'm still doing the laundry, still getting the meals prepared, still helping them transfer from the bed to the wheelchair. I might feel completely drained on the inside, like I'm running on empty, but on the outside, my body is still doing the motions at the same speed. It's not like I'm walking through molasses or anything. It's just that the *effort* to move at that normal speed is taking a lot more out of me. But if you were just watching me work, or listening to me talk to my clients—or even just talking to you right now—I don't think you'd say, \"Wow, he's really moving in slow motion.\" So, no, I wouldn't say there's any actual slowness in my movements or my speech, just the internal heaviness.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, actually, it hasn't quite been a month. I know I might have made it sound like it's been going on longer, probably just because every single day feels so incredibly long and drawn out right now, but... it was really just about two weeks ago. 

And, yeah... you asked if something upsetting happened. There was. My uncle died. It was... man, it was just a really tough situation for everyone. He was my mom's younger brother, and they were really close, so when he passed, it just kind of shattered her. And because of what I do for a living—you know, being a care aide, being used to dealing with medical situations and just managing people's needs—my whole family just kind of defaulted to me to handle everything. I had to be the strong one, the one holding it all together. 

I was the one making the calls to the funeral home, driving over to his apartment, packing up decades of his life into these little cardboard boxes, all while trying to make sure my mom was actually eating and sleeping. And I was doing all of that on top of still going to work every day for Mr. Henderson and Mrs. Gable, because I couldn't just abandon my clients. Like I said, they really rely on me. So I was just running on pure adrenaline, I think, just pushing and pushing through the exhaustion to get my family through the service and the burial. 

But then the funeral ended, the relatives went home, everyone went back to their normal routines, and... that's when it hit me. This whole shift really started right after my uncle died, about two weeks ago. Once the immediate crisis was over and I didn't have to be in go-mode anymore, it was like my brain and my body just completely gave out. That's exactly when that heavy, foggy feeling I've been telling you about just settled right over me, and it just hasn't lifted since. So, yeah, it was two weeks ago, not a month, but that was definitely the turning point.

I... gosh, I'm really not sure. I don't think so? Or at least, I haven't really noticed feeling anything like that. 

It's weird because I know I *should* be having those moments. He was a really great guy. He was always the one who brought this incredibly loud, electric carving knife to Thanksgiving every single year since I was a teenager, and he'd make this whole big show of carving the turkey. And I know that's a good memory. I know I loved that about him. He was always doing stuff like that, or slipping me a twenty-dollar bill when I was younger, just always trying to make people smile. But when I try to picture those things right now... I don't really feel any warmth from it. I just feel... blank, I guess. 

Instead of feeling the good memory, my brain just immediately loops back to the logistics. Like, I'll think about the electric knife, and then I just remember finding that exact knife in the back of his kitchen cabinet two weeks ago. It was shoved way in the back behind this stack of old bowling trophies he had from a league in the 90s, and I just stood there in his kitchen for what felt like an hour, staring at the trophies and the knife, trying to figure out if my mom would want to keep them or if I should put them in the donation pile. 

So, yeah, I haven't really noticed any positive feelings breaking through. It's like I was saying earlier about that fog. The fog just kind of coats everything right now, even the past. When I pull up a memory of him, I don't feel the joy of it. I just feel the heavy, exhausting weight of packing up his apartment, or I just see the funeral director's face, or the stack of cardboard boxes in his living room. I want to feel those warm memories, I really do, but right now... I just don't know. I'm just not feeling it.

But if you were just watching me work, or listening to me talk to my clients—or even just talking to you right now—I don't think you'd say, \"Wow, he's really moving in slow motion.\" So, no, I wouldn't say there's any actual slowness in my movements or my speech, just the internal heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Right before this started about a month ago, did anything particularly stressful or upsetting happen in your life?\n\nPatient: Well, actually, it hasn't quite been a month. Well, no, not really. I mean, you'd think that being around people would help, right? That's what everyone says—you know, "don't isolate yourself, lean on your support system." And I've certainly been around people. Between work and checking on my mom, I'm rarely completely alone. 

Like, just this past Sunday, my sister came into town to check on my mom, and she brought over this massive tray of baked ziti from that Italian place down on 4th Street—the one with the red and white checkered awnings? We used to go there for every single birthday when we were kids, and that ziti is usually my absolute favorite thing in the world. Normally, just the smell of the garlic and the melted mozzarella would instantly put me in a good mood, no matter how bad my day was. And she even brought my nephews, who are usually just a riot. They're seven and nine, and they were running around the living room, building this huge fort out of my mom's couch cushions and couch blankets, just giggling and being kids. 

And I sat there on the recliner, eating the ziti, watching them play, and my sister was trying to tell me this funny story about how her dog got loose in the neighborhood... and I just felt absolutely nothing. The ziti just tasted like cardboard, honestly. I was chewing it, but there was no comfort in it, no enjoyment. And watching the boys... I knew they were being cute, I knew I should be smiling or laughing with my sister, but it was just like I was watching them through a thick pane of glass. I couldn't connect to the warmth of the room at all. 

So, I don't know... I guess the answer is no. Even when I'm doing something that's supposed to be comforting, or I'm surrounded by my family trying to cheer me up, that heavy, empty feeling just doesn't budge. It's like I'm there physically, but the part of me that actually feels the comfort or the connection is just totally switched off right now.

I usually have a mind like a steel trap because in my line of work, you really can't afford to be scatterbrained. People are relying on you. So having this constant fog, making these little errors, forgetting what I'm doing right in the middle of doing it... it's incredibly frustrating. It makes me feel like I can't trust my own brain right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or talking more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly... I don't think I'm moving or talking slower. I mean, I know I've been talking a lot about how heavy everything feels, that physical exhaustion I mentioned, where it just takes so much effort to get out of bed or make breakfast. But once I'm actually up and moving, especially at work, I have to keep my normal pace. \n\nLike with Mr. Henderson, for example. When we do his physical therapy exercises, there's a specific rhythm to it. If I move too slow, he loses his balance, and if I talk too slow while counting out his reps, he gets confused and frustrated. So I'm still counting, \"One, two, three, lift,\" at the exact same speed I always have. And with Mrs. Gable, she's a bit hard of hearing, so I have to speak up and speak clearly, but I'm not, you know, dragging my words out or sounding like a record played at the wrong speed. \n\nAnd as for moving around... I'm still doing the laundry, still getting the meals prepared, still helping them transfer from the bed to the wheelchair. I might feel completely drained on the inside, like I'm running on empty, but on the outside, my body is still doing the motions at the same speed. It's not like I'm walking through molasses or anything. It's just that the *effort* to move at that normal speed is taking a lot more out of me. But if you were just watching me work, or listening to me talk to my clients—or even just talking to you right now—I don't think you'd say, \"Wow, he's really moving in slow motion.\" So, no, I wouldn't say there's any actual slowness in my movements or my speech, just the internal heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Right before this started about a month ago, did anything particularly stressful or upsetting happen in your life?\n\nPatient: Well, actually, it hasn't quite been a month. I know I might have made it sound like it's been going on longer, probably just because every single day feels so incredibly long and drawn out right now, but... it was really just about two weeks ago. \n\nAnd, yeah... you asked if something upsetting happened. There was. My uncle died. It was... man, it was just a really tough situation for everyone. He was my mom's younger brother, and they were really close, so when he passed, it just kind of shattered her. And because of what I do for a living—you know, being a care aide, being used to dealing with medical situations and just managing people's needs—my whole family just kind of defaulted to me to handle everything. I had to be the strong one, the one holding it all together. \n\nI was the one making the calls to the funeral home, driving over to his apartment, packing up decades of his life into these little cardboard boxes, all while trying to make sure my mom was actually eating and sleeping. So I was just running on pure adrenaline, I think, just pushing and pushing through the exhaustion to get my family through the service and the burial. \n\nBut then the funeral ended, the relatives went home, everyone went back to their normal routines, and... that's when it hit me. This whole shift really started right after my uncle died, about two weeks ago. Once the immediate crisis was over and I didn't have to be in go-mode anymore, it was like my brain and my body just completely gave out. That's exactly when that heavy, foggy feeling I've been telling you about just settled right over me, and it just hasn't lifted since. So, yeah, it was two weeks ago, not a month, but that was definitely the turning point.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your uncle, are you still able to feel any warm or positive memories, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I... gosh, I'm really not sure. I don't think so? Or at least, I haven't really noticed feeling anything like that. \n\nIt's weird because I know I *should* be having those moments. He was a really great guy. He was always the one who brought this incredibly loud, electric carving knife to Thanksgiving every single year since I was a teenager, and he'd make this whole big show of carving the turkey. And I know that's a good memory. I know I loved that about him. He was always doing stuff like that, or slipping me a twenty-dollar bill when I was younger, just always trying to make people smile. But when I try to picture those things right now... I don't really feel any warmth from it. I just feel... blank, I guess. \n\nInstead of feeling the good memory, my brain just immediately loops back to the logistics. Like, I'll think about the electric knife, and then I just remember finding that exact knife in the back of his kitchen cabinet two weeks ago. It was shoved way in the back behind this stack of old bowling trophies he had from a league in the 90s, and I just stood there in his kitchen for what felt like an hour, staring at the trophies and the knife, trying to figure out if my mom would want to keep them or if I should put them in the donation pile. \n\nSo, yeah, I haven't really noticed any positive feelings breaking through. I just feel the heavy, exhausting weight of packing up his apartment, or I just see the funeral director's face, or the stack of cardboard boxes in his living room. I want to feel those warm memories, I really do, but right now... I just don't know. I'm just not feeling it.\n\nDoctor: Since your uncle died, have there been any moments when being with other people or doing something comforting has briefly lifted your mood, even a little?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. Between work and checking on my mom, I'm rarely completely alone. \n\nLike, just this past Sunday, my sister came into town to check on my mom, and she brought over this massive tray of baked ziti from that Italian place down on 4th Street—the one with the red and white checkered awnings? We used to go there for every single birthday when we were kids, and that ziti is usually my absolute favorite thing in the world. Normally, just the smell of the garlic and the melted mozzarella would instantly put me in a good mood, no matter how bad my day was. And she even brought my nephews, who are usually just a riot. They're seven and nine, and they were running around the living room, building this huge fort out of my mom's couch cushions and couch blankets, just giggling and being kids. \n\nAnd I sat there on the recliner, eating the ziti, watching them play, and my sister was trying to tell me this funny story about how her dog got loose in the neighborhood... and I just felt absolutely nothing. I couldn't connect to the warmth of the room at all. \n\nSo, I don't know... I guess the answer is no. No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my brain definitely feels like it's wading through thick mud right now, just with that heavy fog I was talking about, but the actual facts and figures? They're all still locked in there. I haven't forgotten anyone's name or where I live or anything major like that. 

Honestly, with what I do for a living, I really can't afford to let things slip through the cracks, so my brain is kind of trained to hold onto schedules. Like with Mr. Henderson, one of my main clients, he has this very strict medication regimen. He takes his blood pressure pill at exactly 8:00 AM, and then he has a water pill at noon, and if I forget those, it throws his whole day off. And Mrs. Gable has her mobility exercises every Tuesday and Thursday at 1:15 PM sharp. I haven't missed a single one of those, even with everything that's happened over the last couple of weeks. 

And even with my family, I'm the one remembering all the details for my mom right now. I remember the name of the funeral director—Mr. Abernathy, over at the place on Elm Street, right past the old post office—and I've got all my uncle's account passwords written down, but I actually have most of them memorized already just because I've had to log into them so many times to help settle his bills. I can even tell you the exact address of his apartment building, it's 442 West Oak, apartment 3B. 

So, no, the memory part is completely intact. I know exactly where I'm supposed to be, I know my address, I know who everyone is. It's just that going to those appointments and seeing those people feels like I'm dragging a hundred-pound weight behind me. But the information itself? No, I haven't forgotten any of that. I'm still keeping the whole schedule running for everyone, even if I feel totally empty doing it.
